I just don't really want to stop, or maybe it's that stopping doesn't even cross my mind while it's happening. \n\nI'll be standing there, and the house is so quiet, you know? Just the hum of the refrigerator. And I'll open a box of crackers, and I'll eat one, and then another, and I'm just sort of staring out the window into the dark backyard. And it's almost like a trance, but not quite. I'm aware I'm doing it. I know I'm eating the crackers, and then maybe I'll move on to a handful of chocolate chips from the baking shelf, but it's not this frantic, out-of-control feeling. It's more like a soothing rhythm. Hand to mouth, chew, swallow. Hand to mouth. \n\nI suppose if the phone rang, or if someone came downstairs and turned on the kitchen light and said, \"What are you doing?\" I could easily put the box down and walk away. I wouldn't fight them for the crackers, you know? So, no, I don't feel like I absolutely cannot stop. It's just that in those moments, in the quiet and the dark, I just sort of... keep going. Until I finally realize my stomach feels a bit too full, or my feet get cold on the linoleum, and then I just close the pantry door and go back up to bed. So, no, not out of control, just... continuous, I guess you could say.\n\nDoctor: Are you very worried about gaining weight or about the shape of your body?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I mean, it's funny you bring that up because my sister, she's always on some new diet, always talking about carbs and waistlines and standing in front of the mirror pinching her sides, and I just... I don't really do that. I know I was just talking about the eating at night, the crackers and the chocolate chips and all, and you'd think that would make me obsess over the scale, but honestly, I hardly ever even look at it. \n\nThe scale is actually just gathering dust in the corner of my bathroom right now, right next to this little wicker wastebasket I bought at a craft fair years ago. I just don't have that kind of anxiety about my shape. I'm forty-six, you know? I'm not trying to fit into a bikini or impress anyone. I have my comfortable clothes, my loose sweaters, and my soft pants that I wear around the house, and as long as they fit, I don't give it much thought. \n\nSometimes I'll catch my reflection in the hallway mirror—the big one with the oak frame that we got from my husband's aunt—and I just see... me. Just a person. I don't pick myself apart. I'm much more concerned about that heavy feeling I was telling you about earlier, that grayness, than I am about whether my thighs are getting a little thicker or my stomach isn't perfectly flat. It's just... the outside doesn't seem to matter all that much when the inside feels so hollow, if that makes any sense. So, no, I wouldn't say I'm worried about gaining weight at all. It just isn't really on my radar.\n\nDoctor: During those heavier periods, does the sadness or emptiness last most of the day nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Well, yes. Yes, I'd say so. It’s... it’s not just a fleeting thing, you know? It’s not like I wake up feeling a little down because it’s raining and then by lunchtime I’m fine. It really settles in. I go through these stretches—and they definitely last for weeks at a time, sometimes longer—where the whole house just feels... gray. And heavy. It’s the strangest sensation, like the air itself is thicker and harder to move through. \n\nI’ll just sit there at the kitchen table, the one by the window where I usually like to have my morning tea, and I’ll just feel so incredibly empty. And the confusing part is that nothing terrible has even happened that day. The mail came, the birds are at the feeder, everything is perfectly normal, but inside I just feel this profound, hollow sadness. I’ll sit there staring at the grain of the wood on the table—it’s this lovely old oak table we got at an estate sale years ago, and it has all these interesting knots and swirls in it—and I’ll just trace them with my finger and feel completely drained of any joy or energy. \n\nIt’s almost like someone turned the color dial all the way down on my life. And it’s not just for an hour or two; it’s most of the day, pretty much every single day during those periods. I’ll try to get up and do something, maybe sort through the mail or water the spider plant in the living room, but the heaviness just follows me from room to room. So, yes, when those periods hit, they really take hold and linger for quite a while.\n\nDoctor: During those gray periods, do you lose interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy, like your art?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, absolutely. You mentioned art, and it’s so true. I used to love going down to the galleries in the arts district—there's this one little cooperative on 4th Street that always has the most wonderful local watercolors, and I used to make a whole afternoon of it. I'd get a coffee, stroll through, talk to the owners. But during those heavy weeks, the thought of going just feels so exhausting. \n\nAnd it’s not just the art, really, it's everything. Things I used to look forward to just completely lose their spark. Take my book club, for instance. We meet every other Tuesday, usually at my friend Sarah's house because she has this gorgeous, bright sunroom with all these wicker chairs, and I used to be the first one to finish the monthly book. I'd even bake something to bring, like my lemon blueberry scones, which everyone always asks for. But when that grayness settles in, I just stare at the book sitting on my nightstand. I don't even want to open the cover. It just feels like a massive chore that I keep putting off, week after week, until I'm making up excuses not to go.\n\nIt's the same with cooking. I usually love being in the kitchen, chopping vegetables, trying out new recipes I found in magazines, simmering sauces... but then it just becomes this monumental task. Standing at the stove just feels like work. \n\nEven my rose beds! I have these beautiful apricot-colored roses out back, and they need regular pruning and deadheading, which I usually find so incredibly therapeutic. You know, getting my hands in the dirt, feeling the sunshine on my back, listening to the neighborhood wake up. But when I'm in that gray place, I look out the kitchen window at them and I just think, 'Ugh, another thing I have to do.' I just keep putting it off and putting it off until the petals are all browning and dropping onto the soil. It's like all the joy is just siphoned right out of the things that make me *me*, and everything just turns into this endless list of obligations I want to hide from.\n\nDoctor: Even when things at home are relatively calm, do you still go through those gray, heavy periods?\n\nPatient: Well, that's the thing, yes. I do. It doesn't really seem to matter if the house is perfectly quiet and everything is fine. Like, last month, my husband was away on a fishing trip with his brother up at Lake George—they go every year, and they always bring back these stories about the weather or the boat motor acting up—so it was just me here. The house was spotless, the bills were all paid, there was absolutely no stress at all. I even had this whole plan to finally organize the hall closet, the one where we just throw all the winter coats and the extra blankets and those old board games we never play anymore. I thought, 'Oh, this will be a nice, quiet weekend to just get things done at my own pace.' \n\nBut then, out of nowhere, that heavy, gray feeling just rolled in like a fog. It was a Tuesday, I think, or maybe Wednesday, and I was just sitting in the living room, looking at the sunlight coming through the blinds and making those little striped patterns on the rug. And I just couldn't move. There was no reason for it. No arguments, no bad news, no stress. Just... quiet. But the sadness was so overwhelming. I sat there for hours, just watching the dust motes float in the air, feeling completely hollowed out. \n\nIt's almost more frustrating when it happens during the calm times, you know? Because when things are chaotic or stressful, at least you can point to something and say, 'Oh, that's why I feel this way.' But when everything is fine, when there's no reason to be sad, and you still feel like you're dragging a boulder behind you... it just makes you feel so broken. So, yes, even when things are perfectly calm, those gray periods still find me. They just invite themselves right in and unpack their bags.\n\nDoctor: During those gray periods, have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living?\n\nPatient: Well... yes. Yes, I suppose I have. It’s hard to admit, really, because it sounds so terrible when you say it out loud, especially when, like I was just saying, there’s no real reason for me to feel this way. But when the grayness gets so thick, when it just settles into every corner of the house and every corner of my mind... it just feels endless. \n\nEspecially at night. The nights are the hardest. My husband will be asleep, breathing so steadily next to me, and the house is perfectly quiet—just the hum of the refrigerator or maybe the wind rattling that one loose pane in the bedroom window, the one we’ve been meaning to fix since last autumn. And I’ll just lie there, staring at the ceiling, watching the shadows from the streetlamp outside shift across the plaster. And in those moments, when I’m just so incredibly tired of feeling so empty and heavy, I do think... I think it would just be so much easier if I didn't wake up the next morning. \n\nIt’s not that I want to die, exactly, it’s just that being alive feels like such a monumental effort. Like dragging that boulder, you know? And the thought of just... slipping away in my sleep, of not having to face another day of that hollow feeling, of not having to force myself to get out of bed and pretend everything is fine... it just seems peaceful. \n\nBut I want to be clear—I haven’t actually made a plan to hurt myself. I haven't done anything like that. I wouldn't do that to my husband, or my sister, who relies on me to help her with her garden in the spring. I know it would devastate them. I don't have pills hidden away or anything like that. It's just... a thought that creeps in. A wish, almost, on the very worst nights, that the universe would just quietly take the decision out of my hands so I could finally just rest.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt unusually energetic or high, more than your normal self?\n\nPatient: Oh, yes. It's actually quite strange how different it is from the grayness we were just talking about. It's like... a switch flips, and suddenly I have all this energy. It's not just normal energy, either. I feel completely bright, you know? Like I'm just buzzing with ideas and I feel totally unstoppable. I'll be pulling out all my canvases and brushes, setting up in the dining room—which I know drives my husband a little crazy because it takes up the whole table—but I just feel like I have to get it all out, like I can conquer the world and paint a masterpiece all in one afternoon. \n\nBut the thing is, and this is what's really confusing about it, is that it can turn so quickly. I'll be flying high, feeling so wonderful and productive, and then something tiny will happen and I just... I\n\nDoctor: When you have those unusually energetic or high periods, do they ever last 4 days or longer?\n\nPatient: Oh, I don't know about four days. That's... well, it's hard to put an exact number on it, you know? Time gets a little funny when I'm like that. I mean, I'm so caught up in whatever I'm doing, whether it's the painting or deciding I need to completely reorganize the kitchen cabinets—which I did do once, pulled every single dish and mug out and laid them on the floor, sorting them by color and size, and I even started wiping down the shelves with this special lemon oil I bought at the hardware store three towns over because I just had to have that exact scent. I remember my husband coming home and looking at the mess, and I just kept talking and talking about how this new system was going to change our lives. \n\nBut as for how many days exactly... I'm not sure. I don't think I've ever really sat down and tracked it on a calendar. It feels like it lasts a while, because I get so much done, or at least I start a lot of things. But then the irritability creeps in, like I was saying, and everything gets so messy and confusing. So, four days? Maybe? Or maybe it's just a weekend? I really haven't noticed a specific timeframe like that. It just happens, and I ride the wave until it crashes.\n\nDoctor: During those unusually energetic periods, do you need much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. It’s incredible, really, because normally I love my sleep, especially when I’m in one of those gray slumps where I feel like I could stay under the covers for a week. But when I’m feeling bright and energetic, it’s like my body just entirely forgets how to be tired. \n\nI mean, I’ll finally force myself to go to bed at maybe one or two in the morning because I’ve been so busy with my painting or whatever else I've gotten myself into, but then... I can sleep just three or four hours, tops, and I'll just pop right awake. And I don't wake up groggy or dragging my feet at all. I wake up feeling completely charged. It’s like electricity is running through my veins. \n\nIt’ll be 4:30 in the morning, pitch black outside, the whole neighborhood is dead silent, and I’m already out of bed, making a pot of coffee, and I'm just ready to go. I'll sit down at the computer and start writing these incredibly long blog posts—I have this little blog about upcycling furniture and gardening that I usually completely neglect—but suddenly I have so much to say, and I'm drafting three or four posts before the sun is even up. Or I’ll be planning out all these massive new projects, sitting at the kitchen table drawing diagrams for a completely new landscaping design for the backyard, making lists of seeds and soil I need to order. \n\nMy husband will come downstairs at seven, rubbing his eyes, barely awake, and I’ve already been up for hours, feeling like I’ve accomplished a whole day's worth of work. You’d think I’d crash, you know? You’d think anyone would be exhausted by noon after only getting three hours of sleep, but I just don't feel it. I just keep going and going. I actually kind of love those early mornings because no one is awake to interrupt me, but my mind is just racing with all these brilliant ideas...\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Oh, yes, absolutely. It's... well, it's actually a wonderful feeling, to be honest. It's like all the self-doubt just completely evaporates. Normally, I second-guess everything, you know? But when I'm in one of those bright phases, I feel like I have this incredible clarity and purpose.

I'll start telling my husband about it, explaining how we need to take out a loan because this is going to be a huge success, a total breakthrough. I feel so powerful, like I have this unique gift that no one else has, and it's my absolute duty to share it with the world right that very second. I don't even worry about the fact that I'm not technically qualified yet, because in those moments, I feel like my natural intuition is far superior to any piece of paper or training program. It's just this overwhelming sense of... destiny, I guess you could call it.

And it can last for days like that, where I'm just dragging myself from room to room, feeling like a ghost in my own house. I try to shake it off, I really do. I'll tell myself to snap out of it, but it's like trying to walk through deep water. It just takes so much effort just to exist when it gets like that.\n\nDoctor: Did these problems start after a specific stressful event or major life change?\n\nPatient: Well, it's funny you ask about a stressful event, because I've thought about that a lot. I really have. I've tried to trace it all back, like following a string through a maze, just trying to find where it all started. And, you know, it hasn't always been like this. I mean, I've always been a little up and down, but this... this specific feeling, this heaviness and the eating and all of it, it's been going on for a while now. I'd say... gosh, it's been about two years and five months. \n\nI know that sounds oddly specific, but I remember it so clearly because it was right around the time things at home just got... well, they got really bad. It was the arguments. We were just fighting all the time, mostly about spending, which is always such a horrible thing to argue about, isn't it? It just makes everyone feel so defensive and tense. But it wasn't just the money. It was my art projects. I love my art, I really do, and I pour so much of myself into it, but it became this huge point of contention. The arguments just kept escalating and escalating, and the house felt like a pressure cooker. You know that feeling where you're just waiting for the next explosion? That's what it was like. And it was right around then, when the fighting was at its worst, that I started noticing all these changes in myself. So, yes, I suppose you could say it started after a major stress, because that whole period was just incredibly overwhelming, and I don't think I ever really bounced back from it.\n\nDoctor: When those times happen in the kitchen at night, do you feel like you can't stop eating once you've started?\n\nPatient: Well, that's... I mean, that's a really interesting way to put it. I guess I wouldn't say I feel like I *can't* stop, exactly. It's not like a compulsion where my hands are moving on their own and I'm trapped inside my body watching it happen. It's more like... I just don't really want to stop, or maybe it's that stopping doesn't even cross my mind while it's happening. \n\nI'll be standing there, and the house is so quiet, you know? Just the hum of the refrigerator. And I'll open a box of crackers, and I'll eat one, and then another, and I'm just sort of staring out the window into the dark backyard. And it's almost like a trance, but not quite. I'm aware I'm doing it. I know I'm eating the crackers, and then maybe I'll move on to a handful of chocolate chips from the baking shelf, but it's not this frantic, out-of-control feeling. It's more like a soothing rhythm. Hand to mouth, chew, swallow. Hand to mouth. \n\nI suppose if the phone rang, or if someone came downstairs and turned on the kitchen light and said, \"What are you doing?\" I could easily put the box down and walk away. I wouldn't fight them for the crackers, you know? So, no, I don't feel like I absolutely cannot stop. It's just that in those moments, in the quiet and the dark, I just sort of... keep going. Until I finally realize my stomach feels a bit too full, or my feet get cold on the linoleum, and then I just close the pantry door and go back up to bed. So, no, not out of control, just... continuous, I guess you could say.\n\nDoctor: Are you very worried about gaining weight or about the shape of your body?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I mean, it's funny you bring that up because my sister, she's always on some new diet, always talking about carbs and waistlines and standing in front of the mirror pinching her sides, and I just... I don't really do that. I know I was just talking about the eating at night, the crackers and the chocolate chips and all, and you'd think that would make me obsess over the scale, but honestly, I hardly ever even look at it. \n\nThe scale is actually just gathering dust in the corner of my bathroom right now, right next to this little wicker wastebasket I bought at a craft fair years ago. I just don't have that kind of anxiety about my shape. I'm forty-six, you know? I'm not trying to fit into a bikini or impress anyone. I have my comfortable clothes, my loose sweaters, and my soft pants that I wear around the house, and as long as they fit, I don't give it much thought. \n\nSometimes I'll catch my reflection in the hallway mirror—the big one with the oak frame that we got from my husband's aunt—and I just see... me. Just a person. I don't pick myself apart. I'm much more concerned about that heavy feeling I was telling you about earlier, that grayness, than I am about whether my thighs are getting a little thicker or my stomach isn't perfectly flat. It's just... the outside doesn't seem to matter all that much when the inside feels so hollow, if that makes any sense. So, no, I wouldn't say I'm worried about gaining weight at all. It just isn't really on my radar.\n\nDoctor: During those heavier periods, does the sadness or emptiness last most of the day nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Well, yes. Yes, I'd say so. It’s... it’s not just a fleeting thing, you know? It’s not like I wake up feeling a little down because it’s raining and then by lunchtime I’m fine. It really settles in. I go through these stretches—and they definitely last for weeks at a time, sometimes longer—where the whole house just feels... gray. And heavy. It’s the strangest sensation, like the air itself is thicker and harder to move through. \n\nI’ll just sit there at the kitchen table, the one by the window where I usually like to have my morning tea, and I’ll just feel so incredibly empty. And the confusing part is that nothing terrible has even happened that day. The mail came, the birds are at the feeder, everything is perfectly normal, but inside I just feel this profound, hollow sadness. I’ll sit there staring at the grain of the wood on the table—it’s this lovely old oak table we got at an estate sale years ago, and it has all these interesting knots and swirls in it—and I’ll just trace them with my finger and feel completely drained of any joy or energy. \n\nIt’s almost like someone turned the color dial all the way down on my life. And it’s not just for an hour or two; it’s most of the day, pretty much every single day during those periods. I’ll try to get up and do something, maybe sort through the mail or water the spider plant in the living room, but the heaviness just follows me from room to room. So, yes, when those periods hit, they really take hold and linger for quite a while.\n\nDoctor: During those gray periods, do you lose interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy, like your art?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, absolutely. You mentioned art, and it’s so true. I used to love going down to the galleries in the arts district—there's this one little cooperative on 4th Street that always has the most wonderful local watercolors, and I used to make a whole afternoon of it. I'd get a coffee, stroll through, talk to the owners. But during those heavy weeks, the thought of going just feels so exhausting. \n\nAnd it’s not just the art, really, it's everything. Things I used to look forward to just completely lose their spark. Take my book club, for instance. We meet every other Tuesday, usually at my friend Sarah's house because she has this gorgeous, bright sunroom with all these wicker chairs, and I used to be the first one to finish the monthly book. I'd even bake something to bring, like my lemon blueberry scones, which everyone always asks for. But when that grayness settles in, I just stare at the book sitting on my nightstand. I don't even want to open the cover. It just feels like a massive chore that I keep putting off, week after week, until I'm making up excuses not to go.\n\nIt's the same with cooking. I usually love being in the kitchen, chopping vegetables, trying out new recipes I found in magazines, simmering sauces... but then it just becomes this monumental task. Standing at the stove just feels like work. \n\nEven my rose beds! I have these beautiful apricot-colored roses out back, and they need regular pruning and deadheading, which I usually find so incredibly therapeutic. You know, getting my hands in the dirt, feeling the sunshine on my back, listening to the neighborhood wake up. But when I'm in that gray place, I look out the kitchen window at them and I just think, 'Ugh, another thing I have to do.' I just keep putting it off and putting it off until the petals are all browning and dropping onto the soil. It's like all the joy is just siphoned right out of the things that make me *me*, and everything just turns into this endless list of obligations I want to hide from.\n\nDoctor: Even when things at home are relatively calm, do you still go through those gray, heavy periods?\n\nPatient: Well, that's the thing, yes. I do. It doesn't really seem to matter if the house is perfectly quiet and everything is fine. Like, last month, my husband was away on a fishing trip with his brother up at Lake George—they go every year, and they always bring back these stories about the weather or the boat motor acting up—so it was just me here. The house was spotless, the bills were all paid, there was absolutely no stress at all. I even had this whole plan to finally organize the hall closet, the one where we just throw all the winter coats and the extra blankets and those old board games we never play anymore. I thought, 'Oh, this will be a nice, quiet weekend to just get things done at my own pace.' \n\nBut then, out of nowhere, that heavy, gray feeling just rolled in like a fog. It was a Tuesday, I think, or maybe Wednesday, and I was just sitting in the living room, looking at the sunlight coming through the blinds and making those little striped patterns on the rug. And I just couldn't move. There was no reason for it. No arguments, no bad news, no stress. Just... quiet. But the sadness was so overwhelming. I sat there for hours, just watching the dust motes float in the air, feeling completely hollowed out. \n\nIt's almost more frustrating when it happens during the calm times, you know? Because when things are chaotic or stressful, at least you can point to something and say, 'Oh, that's why I feel this way.' But when everything is fine, when there's no reason to be sad, and you still feel like you're dragging a boulder behind you... it just makes you feel so broken. So, yes, even when things are perfectly calm, those gray periods still find me. They just invite themselves right in and unpack their bags.\n\nDoctor: During those gray periods, have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living?\n\nPatient: Well... yes. Yes, I suppose I have. It’s hard to admit, really, because it sounds so terrible when you say it out loud, especially when, like I was just saying, there’s no real reason for me to feel this way. But when the grayness gets so thick, when it just settles into every corner of the house and every corner of my mind... it just feels endless. \n\nEspecially at night. The nights are the hardest. My husband will be asleep, breathing so steadily next to me, and the house is perfectly quiet—just the hum of the refrigerator or maybe the wind rattling that one loose pane in the bedroom window, the one we’ve been meaning to fix since last autumn. And I’ll just lie there, staring at the ceiling, watching the shadows from the streetlamp outside shift across the plaster. And in those moments, when I’m just so incredibly tired of feeling so empty and heavy, I do think... Like dragging that boulder, you know? And the thought of just... slipping away in my sleep, of not having to face another day of that hollow feeling, of not having to force myself to get out of bed and pretend everything is fine... it just seems peaceful. \n\nBut I want to be clear—I haven’t actually made a plan to hurt myself. I haven't done anything like that. I wouldn't do that to my husband, or my sister, who relies on me to help her with her garden in the spring. I know it would devastate them. I don't have pills hidden away or anything like that. It's just... a thought that creeps in. A wish, almost, on the very worst nights, that the universe would just quietly take the decision out of my hands so I could finally just rest.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt unusually energetic or high, more than your normal self?\n\nPatient: Oh, yes. It's actually quite strange how different it is from the grayness we were just talking about. It's like... a switch flips, and suddenly I have all this energy. It's not just normal energy, either. I feel completely bright, you know? Like I'm just buzzing with ideas and I feel totally unstoppable. I'll be pulling out all my canvases and brushes, setting up in the dining room—which I know drives my husband a little crazy because it takes up the whole table—but I just feel like I have to get it all out, like I can conquer the world and paint a masterpiece all in one afternoon. \n\nBut the thing is, and this is what's really confusing about it, is that it can turn so quickly. I remember my husband coming home and looking at the mess, and I just kept talking and talking about how this new system was going to change our lives. \n\nBut as for how many days exactly... It’s like electricity is running through my veins. \n\nIt’ll be 4:30 in the morning, pitch black outside, the whole neighborhood is dead silent, and I’m already out of bed, making a pot of coffee, and I'm just ready to go. I'll sit down at the computer and start writing these incredibly long blog posts—I have this little blog about upcycling furniture and gardening that I usually completely neglect—but suddenly I have so much to say, and I'm drafting three or four posts before the sun is even up. You’d think I’d crash, you know? You’d think anyone would be exhausted by noon after only getting three hours of sleep, but I just don't feel it. I just keep going and going. I'll start telling my husband about it, explaining how we need to take out a loan because this is going to be a huge success, a total breakthrough. I feel so powerful, like I have this unique gift that no one else has, and it's my absolute duty to share it with the world right that very second. No, no, I've never been in the hospital for being too energetic. I mean, I've never had to pack a bag and go stay in a ward or anything like that. Though, honestly, my husband probably thought about it once or twice! It gets intense, sure, like I was saying with the art therapy business plan and the landscaping diagrams, and I know it can be an awful lot for the people around me to handle. 

My husband—he gets very stressed when I'm buzzing around like that. He'll try to sit me down at the kitchen table, usually right when I'm in the middle of something incredibly important, and he'll make me drink a glass of water and try to get me to just sit still and breathe for a minute. And sometimes I'll argue with him, you know, telling him he's stifling my creativity and that he just doesn't understand my vision. We had this huge argument about the landscaping thing because he said we absolutely could not afford the three pallets of river rock I was trying to order at five in the morning. 

But even when it gets really heated, and I'm just practically vibrating with all these plans and ideas, it's never gotten to the point where anyone had to call an ambulance or a doctor said I needed to be admitted somewhere. I just sort of... stay home. I burn through it at home. Eventually, the energy just sort of fizzles out, or the irritability takes over like I mentioned before, and then the wave crashes, but I've always managed to just stay in my own house while it happens. So, no, no hospitals. I don't think I could handle a hospital anyway, to be perfectly honest. The fluorescent lights, the strict schedules, people telling you when to eat and sleep... it would drive me absolutely up the wall when I'm feeling that inspired and need to be doing things on my own time.

I'll start telling my husband about it, explaining how we need to take out a loan because this is going to be a huge success, a total breakthrough. I feel so powerful, like I have this unique gift that no one else has, and it's my absolute duty to share it with the world right that very second. I don't even worry about the fact that I'm not technically qualified yet, because in those moments, I feel like my natural intuition is far superior to any piece of paper or training program. It's just this overwhelming sense of... destiny, I guess you could call it.\n\nDoctor: During those bright, energetic periods, have you ever had to be hospitalized because of them?\n\nPatient: No, no, I've never been in the hospital for being too energetic. I mean, I've never had to pack a bag and go stay in a ward or anything like that. Though, honestly, my husband probably thought about it once or twice! It gets intense, sure, like I was saying with the art therapy business plan and the landscaping diagrams, and I know it can be an awful lot for the people around me to handle. \n\nMy husband—he gets very stressed when I'm buzzing around like that. Well, I suppose... I mean, you're asking about major trouble, and I wouldn't call it a total catastrophe where we're losing the house or anything, but yes, it does cause a fair amount of friction. Especially with my husband. Like I was saying about that argument over the river rock, that kind of thing happens a lot when I'm in that state. The tension just builds and builds between us because he just wants things to be normal, you know? He wants to have dinner at six, he wants to sit on the couch and watch his shows, he wants to go to bed at ten, and I am just completely out of sync with all of that. It makes it so hard to just keep our normal routines going. The whole household rhythm gets entirely thrown out the window, and we end up snapping at each other because I feel like he's holding me back, and he feels like I'm spinning out of control. 

And then, ironically, even though I feel like I'm accomplishing so much with all that energy, I actually end up falling behind on the things I really should be focusing on. Like my art therapy facilitator training. I told you about how I get these grand plans for the studio and the business, right? But the actual coursework I'm supposed to be doing for the certification? I completely drop the ball on it. 

It’s because my mind is going a million miles an hour, and my projects just become so incredibly hard to organize. I'll sit down to do an assignment for the class, or even just try to do some work on my private blog—the gardening one I mentioned earlier, where I'm drafting all those posts before the sun comes up—but when I actually look at what I'm doing, my explanations are just all over the place. I can't keep my thoughts in a straight line to save my life. I'll start writing a blog post about planting hydrangeas, and suddenly I'm writing four pages about the spiritual meaning of the color blue and how it relates to childhood trauma, and I just can't rein it in. 

So I end up with all these half-finished, messy projects that don't make sense to anyone but me in that exact moment. I'm not getting fired from a job since I don't work outside the house right now, but my personal work, my training, my blog... it all gets completely derailed. I just can't organize any of it. And then, of course, my husband is upset because the dining room table is a disaster zone of papers and paint and business plans that don't make sense, and the tension at home just makes it even harder to get back on track...

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi... yes, it's nice to meet you too. Well, I suppose I've been meaning to talk to someone for a little while now, just trying to figure things out. Some weeks I sit down at the table and I barely even touch my dinner. I just push the food around my plate, and I have absolutely zero interest in it, even if it's something I usually love to make. But then... it just completely flips. It's so strange because I'm not even sitting down at the table to enjoy it, I'm just standing there eating and eating in the dark. It's really starting to bother me, because I just can't seem to find a normal middle ground anymore, and it's exhausting trying to figure out what my body even wants from one day to the next.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. I mean, it's not every single day, but I definitely go through these... these stretches where everything just feels so heavy. It's hard to explain, but it's like the whole house just feels gray, you know? Like the color has just been drained right out of the walls and the furniture and everything. \n\nI'll just sit there at the kitchen table, maybe with a cup of coffee that's gone completely cold because I forgot to drink it, and I just feel so empty inside. And the strange thing is, I'll sit there trying to figure out why I feel so awful, running through the day in my head, but nothing terrible has even happened. It's just a normal Tuesday or whatever, but I feel this weight on me that makes it hard to even want to get up and wash the mug. \n\nIt's just so frustrating because I know I should be doing things—laundry, or paying bills, or just anything productive—but instead I'm just sitting there, staring at the grain of the wood on the table, feeling like I'm hollowed out. And it can last for days like that, where I'm just dragging myself from room to room, feeling like a ghost in my own house. I try to shake it off, I really do. I'll tell myself to snap out of it, but it's like trying to walk through deep water. It just takes so much effort just to exist when it gets like that.\n\nDoctor: Did these problems start after a specific stressful event or major life change?\n\nPatient: Well, it's funny you ask about a stressful event, because I've thought about that a lot. I really have. I've tried to trace it all back, like following a string through a maze, just trying to find where it all started. And, you know, it hasn't always been like this. I mean, I've always been a little up and down, but this... this specific feeling, this heaviness and the eating and all of it, it's been going on for a while now. I'd say... gosh, it's been about two years and five months. \n\nI know that sounds oddly specific, but I remember it so clearly because it was right around the time things at home just got... well, they got really bad. It was the arguments. We were just fighting all the time, mostly about spending, which is always such a horrible thing to argue about, isn't it? It just makes everyone feel so defensive and tense. But it wasn't just the money. It was my art projects. I love my art, I really do, and I pour so much of myself into it, but it became this huge point of contention. The arguments just kept escalating and escalating, and the house felt like a pressure cooker. You know that feeling where you're just waiting for the next explosion? That's what it was like. And it was right around then, when the fighting was at its worst, that I started noticing all these changes in myself. So, yes, I suppose you could say it started after a major stress, because that whole period was just incredibly overwhelming, and I don't think I ever really bounced back from it.\n\nDoctor: When those times happen in the kitchen at night, do you feel like you can't stop eating once you've started?\n\nPatient: Well, that's... I mean, that's a really interesting way to put it. I guess I wouldn't say I feel like I *can't* stop, exactly. It's not like a compulsion where my hands are moving on their own and I'm trapped inside my body watching it happen. It's more like... I just don't really want to stop, or maybe it's that stopping doesn't even cross my mind while it's happening. \n\nI'll be standing there, and the house is so quiet, you know? Just the hum of the refrigerator. And I'll open a box of crackers, and I'll eat one, and then another, and I'm just sort of staring out the window into the dark backyard. And it's almost like a trance, but not quite. I'm aware I'm doing it. I know I'm eating the crackers, and then maybe I'll move on to a handful of chocolate chips from the baking shelf, but it's not this frantic, out-of-control feeling. It's more like a soothing rhythm. Hand to mouth, chew, swallow. Hand to mouth. \n\nI suppose if the phone rang, or if someone came downstairs and turned on the kitchen light and said, \"What are you doing?\" I could easily put the box down and walk away. I wouldn't fight them for the crackers, you know? So, no, I don't feel like I absolutely cannot stop. It's just that in those moments, in the quiet and the dark, I just sort of... keep going. Until I finally realize my stomach feels a bit too full, or my feet get cold on the linoleum, and then I just close the pantry door and go back up to bed. So, no, not out of control, just... continuous, I guess you could say.\n\nDoctor: Are you very worried about gaining weight or about the shape of your body?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I mean, it's funny you bring that up because my sister, she's always on some new diet, always talking about carbs and waistlines and standing in front of the mirror pinching her sides, and I just... I don't really do that. I know I was just talking about the eating at night, the crackers and the chocolate chips and all, and you'd think that would make me obsess over the scale, but honestly, I hardly ever even look at it. \n\nThe scale is actually just gathering dust in the corner of my bathroom right now, right next to this little wicker wastebasket I bought at a craft fair years ago. I just don't have that kind of anxiety about my shape. I'm forty-six, you know? I'm not trying to fit into a bikini or impress anyone. I have my comfortable clothes, my loose sweaters, and my soft pants that I wear around the house, and as long as they fit, I don't give it much thought. \n\nSometimes I'll catch my reflection in the hallway mirror—the big one with the oak frame that we got from my husband's aunt—and I just see... me. Just a person. I don't pick myself apart. I'm much more concerned about that heavy feeling I was telling you about earlier, that grayness, than I am about whether my thighs are getting a little thicker or my stomach isn't perfectly flat. It's just... the outside doesn't seem to matter all that much when the inside feels so hollow, if that makes any sense. So, no, I wouldn't say I'm worried about gaining weight at all. It just isn't really on my radar.\n\nDoctor: During those heavier periods, does the sadness or emptiness last most of the day nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Well, yes. Yes, I'd say so. It’s... it’s not just a fleeting thing, you know? It’s not like I wake up feeling a little down because it’s raining and then by lunchtime I’m fine. It really settles in. I go through these stretches—and they definitely last for weeks at a time, sometimes longer—where the whole house just feels... gray. And heavy. It’s the strangest sensation, like the air itself is thicker and harder to move through. \n\nI’ll just sit there at the kitchen table, the one by the window where I usually like to have my morning tea, and I’ll just feel so incredibly empty. And the confusing part is that nothing terrible has even happened that day. The mail came, the birds are at the feeder, everything is perfectly normal, but inside I just feel this profound, hollow sadness. I’ll sit there staring at the grain of the wood on the table—it’s this lovely old oak table we got at an estate sale years ago, and it has all these interesting knots and swirls in it—and I’ll just trace them with my finger and feel completely drained of any joy or energy. \n\nIt’s almost like someone turned the color dial all the way down on my life. And it’s not just for an hour or two; it’s most of the day, pretty much every single day during those periods. I’ll try to get up and do something, maybe sort through the mail or water the spider plant in the living room, but the heaviness just follows me from room to room. So, yes, when those periods hit, they really take hold and linger for quite a while.\n\nDoctor: During those gray periods, do you lose interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy, like your art?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, absolutely. You mentioned art, and it’s so true. I used to love going down to the galleries in the arts district—there's this one little cooperative on 4th Street that always has the most wonderful local watercolors, and I used to make a whole afternoon of it. I'd get a coffee, stroll through, talk to the owners. But during those heavy weeks, the thought of going just feels so exhausting. \n\nAnd it’s not just the art, really, it's everything. Things I used to look forward to just completely lose their spark. Take my book club, for instance. We meet every other Tuesday, usually at my friend Sarah's house because she has this gorgeous, bright sunroom with all these wicker chairs, and I used to be the first one to finish the monthly book. I'd even bake something to bring, like my lemon blueberry scones, which everyone always asks for. But when that grayness settles in, I just stare at the book sitting on my nightstand. I don't even want to open the cover. It just feels like a massive chore that I keep putting off, week after week, until I'm making up excuses not to go.\n\nIt's the same with cooking. I usually love being in the kitchen, chopping vegetables, trying out new recipes I found in magazines, simmering sauces... but then it just becomes this monumental task. Standing at the stove just feels like work. \n\nEven my rose beds! I have these beautiful apricot-colored roses out back, and they need regular pruning and deadheading, which I usually find so incredibly therapeutic. You know, getting my hands in the dirt, feeling the sunshine on my back, listening to the neighborhood wake up. But when I'm in that gray place, I look out the kitchen window at them and I just think, 'Ugh, another thing I have to do.' I just keep putting it off and putting it off until the petals are all browning and dropping onto the soil. It's like all the joy is just siphoned right out of the things that make me *me*, and everything just turns into this endless list of obligations I want to hide from.\n\nDoctor: Even when things at home are relatively calm, do you still go through those gray, heavy periods?\n\nPatient: Well, that's the thing, yes. I do. It doesn't really seem to matter if the house is perfectly quiet and everything is fine. Like, last month, my husband was away on a fishing trip with his brother up at Lake George—they go every year, and they always bring back these stories about the weather or the boat motor acting up—so it was just me here. The house was spotless, the bills were all paid, there was absolutely no stress at all. I even had this whole plan to finally organize the hall closet, the one where we just throw all the winter coats and the extra blankets and those old board games we never play anymore. I thought, 'Oh, this will be a nice, quiet weekend to just get things done at my own pace.' \n\nBut then, out of nowhere, that heavy, gray feeling just rolled in like a fog. It was a Tuesday, I think, or maybe Wednesday, and I was just sitting in the living room, looking at the sunlight coming through the blinds and making those little striped patterns on the rug. And I just couldn't move. There was no reason for it. No arguments, no bad news, no stress. Just... quiet. But the sadness was so overwhelming. I sat there for hours, just watching the dust motes float in the air, feeling completely hollowed out. \n\nIt's almost more frustrating when it happens during the calm times, you know? Because when things are chaotic or stressful, at least you can point to something and say, 'Oh, that's why I feel this way.' But when everything is fine, when there's no reason to be sad, and you still feel like you're dragging a boulder behind you... it just makes you feel so broken. So, yes, even when things are perfectly calm, those gray periods still find me. They just invite themselves right in and unpack their bags.\n\nDoctor: During those gray periods, have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living?\n\nPatient: Well... yes. Yes, I suppose I have. I don't have pills hidden away or anything like that. It's just... a thought that creeps in. A wish, almost, on the very worst nights, that the universe would just quietly take the decision out of my hands so I could finally just rest.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt unusually energetic or high, more than your normal self?\n\nPatient: Oh, yes. Because when things are chaotic or stressful, at least you can point to something and say, 'Oh, that's why I feel this way.' But when everything is fine, when there's no reason to be sad, and you still feel like you're dragging a boulder behind you... it just makes you feel so broken. So, yes, even when things are perfectly calm, those gray periods still find me. They just invite themselves right in and unpack their bags.\n\nDoctor: During those gray periods, have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living?\n\nPatient: Well... yes. Yes, I suppose I have. It’s hard to admit, really, because it sounds so terrible when you say it out loud, especially when, like I was just saying, there’s no real reason for me to feel this way. But when the grayness gets so thick, when it just settles into every corner of the house and every corner of my mind... it just feels endless. \n\nEspecially at night. The nights are the hardest. My husband will be asleep, breathing so steadily next to me, and the house is perfectly quiet—just the hum of the refrigerator or maybe the wind rattling that one loose pane in the bedroom window, the one we’ve been meaning to fix since last autumn. And I’ll just lie there, staring at the ceiling, watching the shadows from the streetlamp outside shift across the plaster. And in those moments, when I’m just so incredibly tired of feeling so empty and heavy, I do think... I think it would just be so much easier if I didn't wake up the next morning. \n\nIt’s not that I want to die, exactly, it’s just that being alive feels like such a monumental effort. Like dragging that boulder, you know? And the thought of just... slipping away in my sleep, of not having to face another day of that hollow feeling, of not having to force myself to get out of bed and pretend everything is fine... it just seems peaceful. \n\nBut I want to be clear—I haven’t actually made a plan to hurt myself. I haven't done anything like that. I wouldn't do that to my husband, or my sister, who relies on me to help her with her garden in the spring. I know it would devastate them. I don't have pills hidden away or anything like that. It's just... a thought that creeps in. A wish, almost, on the very worst nights, that the universe would just quietly take the decision out of my hands so I could finally just rest.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt unusually energetic or high, more than your normal self?\n\nPatient: Oh, yes. It's actually quite strange how different it is from the grayness we were just talking about. It's like... a switch flips, and suddenly I have all this energy. It's not just normal energy, either. I feel completely bright, you know? Like I'm just buzzing with ideas and I feel totally unstoppable. I'll be pulling out all my canvases and brushes, setting up in the dining room—which I know drives my husband a little crazy because it takes up the whole table—but I just feel like I have to get it all out, like I can conquer the world and paint a masterpiece all in one afternoon. \n\nBut the thing is, and this is what's really confusing about it, is that it can turn so quickly. I'll be flying high, feeling so wonderful and productive, and then something tiny will happen and I just... I\n\nDoctor: When you have those unusually energetic or high periods, do they ever last 4 days or longer?\n\nPatient: Oh, I don't know about four days. That's... well, it's hard to put an exact number on it, you know? Time gets a little funny when I'm like that. I mean, I'm so caught up in whatever I'm doing, whether it's the painting or deciding I need to completely reorganize the kitchen cabinets—which I did do once, pulled every single dish and mug out and laid them on the floor, sorting them by color and size, and I even started wiping down the shelves with this special lemon oil I bought at the hardware store three towns over because I just had to have that exact scent. I remember my husband coming home and looking at the mess, and I just kept talking and talking about how this new system was going to change our lives. \n\nBut as for how many days exactly... I'm not sure. I don't think I've ever really sat down and tracked it on a calendar. It feels like it lasts a while, because I get so much done, or at least I start a lot of things. But then the irritability creeps in, like I was saying, and everything gets so messy and confusing. So, four days? Maybe? Or maybe it's just a weekend? I really haven't noticed a specific timeframe like that. It just happens, and I ride the wave until it crashes.\n\nDoctor: During those unusually energetic periods, do you need much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. It’s incredible, really, because normally I love my sleep, especially when I’m in one of those gray slumps where I feel like I could stay under the covers for a week. But when I’m feeling bright and energetic, it’s like my body just entirely forgets how to be tired. \n\nI mean, I’ll finally force myself to go to bed at maybe one or two in the morning because I’ve been so busy with my painting or whatever else I've gotten myself into, but then... I can sleep just three or four hours, tops, and I'll just pop right awake. And I don't wake up groggy or dragging my feet at all. I wake up feeling completely charged. It’s like electricity is running through my veins. \n\nIt’ll be 4:30 in the morning, pitch black outside, the whole neighborhood is dead silent, and I’m already out of bed, making a pot of coffee, and I'm just ready to go. I'll sit down at the computer and start writing these incredibly long blog posts—I have this little blog about upcycling furniture and gardening that I usually completely neglect—but suddenly I have so much to say, and I'm drafting three or four posts before the sun is even up. Or I’ll be planning out all these massive new projects, sitting at the kitchen table drawing diagrams for a completely new landscaping design for the backyard, making lists of seeds and soil I need to order. \n\nMy husband will come downstairs at seven, rubbing his eyes, barely awake, and I’ve already been up for hours, feeling like I’ve accomplished a whole day's worth of work. You’d think I’d crash, you know? You’d think anyone would be exhausted by noon after only getting three hours of sleep, but I just don't feel it. I just keep going and going. I actually kind of love those early mornings because no one is awake to interrupt me, but my mind is just racing with all these brilliant ideas...\n\nDoctor: During those bright, energetic periods, do you feel unusually confident or powerful, like you can do things other people couldn’t?\n\nPatient: Oh, yes, absolutely. It's... well, it's actually a wonderful feeling, to be honest. It's like all the self-doubt just completely evaporates. Normally, I second-guess everything, you know? But when I'm in one of those bright phases, I feel like I have this incredible clarity and purpose. \n\nLike, for example, I've been taking these online courses for art therapy—just a few classes here and there, nothing official yet, I haven't even finished the basic certification—but when I get like that, I become absolutely convinced that I'm ready to launch this massive program. I'll sit there, at the dining room table with all my paints, and I'll start drafting these elaborate business plans and curriculum guides. I become completely sure that my art-therapy workshop is going to change people's lives in a major, profound way. \n\nI mean, I can just see it so clearly in my head. I imagine renting out this beautiful studio space downtown with exposed brick and big windows, and I can picture the people coming in, and I just know, without a shadow of a doubt, that I have the exact insight and talent to heal them through art. I'll start telling my husband about it, explaining how we need to take out a loan because this is going to be a huge success, a total breakthrough. I feel so powerful, like I have this unique gift that no one else has, and it's my absolute duty to share it with the world right that very second. I don't even worry about the fact that I'm not technically qualified yet, because in those moments, I feel like my natural intuition is far superior to any piece of paper or training program. It's just this overwhelming sense of... destiny, I guess you could call it.\n\nDoctor: During those bright, energetic periods, have you ever had to be hospitalized because of them?\n\nPatient: No, no, I've never been in the hospital for being too energetic. I mean, I've never had to pack a bag and go stay in a ward or anything like that. Though, honestly, my husband probably thought about it once or twice! It gets intense, sure, like I was saying with the art therapy business plan and the landscaping diagrams, and I know it can be an awful lot for the people around me to handle. \n\nMy husband—he gets very stressed when I'm buzzing around like that. He'll try to sit me down at the kitchen table, usually right when I'm in the middle of something incredibly important, and he'll make me drink a glass of water and try to get me to just sit still and breathe for a minute. And sometimes I'll argue with him, you know, telling him he's stifling my creativity and that he just doesn't understand my vision. We had this huge argument about the landscaping thing because he said we absolutely could not afford the three pallets of river rock I was trying to order at five in the morning. \n\nBut even when it gets really heated, and I'm just practically vibrating with all these plans and ideas, it's never gotten to the point where anyone had to call an ambulance or a doctor said I needed to be admitted somewhere. I just sort of... stay home. I burn through it at home. Eventually, the energy just sort of fizzles out, or the irritability takes over like I mentioned before, and then the wave crashes, but I've always managed to just stay in my own house while it happens. So, no, no hospitals. I don't think I could handle a hospital anyway, to be perfectly honest. The fluorescent lights, the strict schedules, people telling you when to eat and sleep... it would drive me absolutely up the wall when I'm feeling that inspired and need to be doing things on my own time.\n\nDoctor: During those bright, energetic periods, do they ever cause serious problems in your life, like major trouble at home, work, or with money?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose... I mean, you're asking about major trouble, and I wouldn't call it a total catastrophe where we're losing the house or anything, but yes, it does cause a fair amount of friction. Especially with my husband. Like I was saying about that argument over the river rock, that kind of thing happens a lot when I'm in that state. The tension just builds and builds between us because he just wants things to be normal, you know? He wants to have dinner at six, he wants to sit on the couch and watch his shows, he wants to go to bed at ten, and I am just completely out of sync with all of that. It makes it so hard to just keep our normal routines going. The whole household rhythm gets entirely thrown out the window, and we end up snapping at each other because I feel like he's holding me back, and he feels like I'm spinning out of control. \n\nAnd then, ironically, even though I feel like I'm accomplishing so much with all that energy, I actually end up falling behind on the things I really should be focusing on. Like my art therapy facilitator training. I told you about how I get these grand plans for the studio and the business, right? But the actual coursework I'm supposed to be doing for the certification? I completely drop the ball on it. \n\nIt’s because my mind is going a million miles an hour, and my projects just become so incredibly hard to organize. I'll sit down to do an assignment for the class, or even just try to do some work on my private blog—the gardening one I mentioned earlier, where I'm drafting all those posts before the sun comes up—but when I actually look at what I'm doing, my explanations are just all over the place. I can't keep my thoughts in a straight line to save my life. I'll start writing a blog post about planting hydrangeas, and suddenly I'm writing four pages about the spiritual meaning of the color blue and how it relates to childhood trauma, and I just can't rein it in. \n\nSo I end up with all these half-finished, messy projects that don't make sense to anyone but me in that exact moment. I'm not getting fired from a job since I don't work outside the house right now, but my personal work, my training, my blog... it all gets completely derailed. I just can't organize any of it. And then, of course, my husband is upset because the dining room table is a disaster zone of papers and paint and business plans that don't make sense, and the tension at home just makes it even harder to get back on track...\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well... I mean, my husband definitely thinks I do. He calls it my overactive imagination, but to me, in the moment, it feels incredibly real and, honestly, quite overwhelming. It really only happens when I’m in those extremely charged, unstoppable stretches—you know, the ones I was just telling you about where I'm planning the art therapy workshops and drafting all those curriculums and sleeping very, very little, maybe just an hour or two a night. My brain is just firing on all cylinders, but that’s also when things start to get a bit... strange. 

For instance, I'll be sitting at the computer in the dark, checking my gardening blog or reading through different online forums, and the comments people leave stop looking like normal comments. They start to feel like coded warnings. Someone will write something completely mundane, like "Make sure to watch out for the frost this weekend," or "I wouldn't plant those too close together," and suddenly I am absolutely convinced that they aren't talking about plants at all. I feel this deep, intense certainty that it's a secret message meant specifically for me, like someone is trying to warn me that I'm in danger or that someone is watching the house. I will sit there for hours, analyzing the timestamps, looking at the spacing of the words, trying to decipher the true meaning behind what they typed. My husband will come out at four in the morning, rub his eyes, and tell me that a comment about mulch is just a comment about mulch, but I just cannot believe him. It feels too real. 

And it's not just the internet, either. During those exact same periods, when the house is completely dead silent and I'm the only one awake, I'll hear things. Well, mostly, I may hear my name when no one is there. It's as clear as day. I'll be hunched over my business plans at the dining room table, and I'll hear someone say my name right over my shoulder. It's so distinct that I'll physically jump out of my chair, sending my papers and pens flying everywhere, and I'll go creeping down the hallway to check the bedroom. But my husband is always fast asleep, snoring away. There's no one else in the house. But I know what I heard. It's so unnerving, but it really only ever seems to happen when I'm in that high-gear, no-sleep mode, spinning all these massive projects. So, yes, I suppose I do believe and experience things then that my husband says are completely unrealistic.

The whole household rhythm gets entirely thrown out the window, and we end up snapping at each other because I feel like he's holding me back, and he feels like I'm spinning out of control. \n\nAnd then, ironically, even though I feel like I'm accomplishing so much with all that energy, I actually end up falling behind on the things I really should be focusing on. Like my art therapy facilitator training. I told you about how I get these grand plans for the studio and the business, right? But the actual coursework I'm supposed to be doing for the certification? I completely drop the ball on it. \n\nIt’s because my mind is going a million miles an hour, and my projects just become so incredibly hard to organize. I'll sit down to do an assignment for the class, or even just try to do some work on my private blog—the gardening one I mentioned earlier, where I'm drafting all those posts before the sun comes up—but when I actually look at what I'm doing, my explanations are just all over the place. I can't keep my thoughts in a straight line to save my life. I'll start writing a blog post about planting hydrangeas, and suddenly I'm writing four pages about the spiritual meaning of the color blue and how it relates to childhood trauma, and I just can't rein it in. \n\nSo I end up with all these half-finished, messy projects that don't make sense to anyone but me in that exact moment. I'm not getting fired from a job since I don't work outside the house right now, but my personal work, my training, my blog... it all gets completely derailed. I just can't organize any of it. And then, of course, my husband is upset because the dining room table is a disaster zone of papers and paint and business plans that don't make sense, and the tension at home just makes it even harder to get back on track...\n\nDoctor: During those bright, energetic periods, have you ever believed something that other people said was clearly not true or unrealistic?\n\nPatient: Well... I mean, my husband definitely thinks I do. He calls it my overactive imagination, but to me, in the moment, it feels incredibly real and, honestly, quite overwhelming. It really only happens when I’m in those extremely charged, unstoppable stretches—you know, the ones I was just telling you about where I'm planning the art therapy workshops and drafting all those curriculums and sleeping very, very little, maybe just an hour or two a night. My brain is just firing on all cylinders, but that’s also when things start to get a bit... strange. \n\nFor instance, I'll be sitting at the computer in the dark, checking my gardening blog or reading through different online forums, and the comments people leave stop looking like normal comments. They start to feel like coded warnings. Someone will write something completely mundane, like \"Make sure to watch out for the frost this weekend,\" or \"I wouldn't plant those too close together,\" and suddenly I am absolutely convinced that they aren't talking about plants at all. I feel this deep, intense certainty that it's a secret message meant specifically for me, like someone is trying to warn me that I'm in danger or that someone is watching the house. I will sit there for hours, analyzing the timestamps, looking at the spacing of the words, trying to decipher the true meaning behind what they typed. My husband will come out at four in the morning, rub his eyes, and tell me that a comment about mulch is just a comment about mulch, but I just cannot believe him. It feels too real. \n\nAnd it's not just the internet, either. During those exact same periods, when the house is completely dead silent and I'm the only one awake, I'll hear things. Well, mostly, I may hear my name when no one is there. It's as clear as day. I'll be hunched over my business plans at the dining room table, and I'll hear someone say my name right over my shoulder. It's so distinct that I'll physically jump out of my chair, sending my papers and pens flying everywhere, and I'll go creeping down the hallway to check the bedroom. But my husband is always fast asleep, snoring away. There's no one else in the house. But I know what I heard. It's so unnerving, but it really only ever seems to happen when I'm in that high-gear, no-sleep mode, spinning all these massive projects. So, yes, I suppose I do believe and experience things then that my husband says are completely unrealistic."
